*Proof.* For <a href="#cold:retirement-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cold:retirement-bound">[cold:retirement-bound]</a>, use $f_i\le m_i$ and the same Gaussian lattice sum as in Lemma <a href="#cold:nonmain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cold:nonmain">18</a>, without extracting a small distance factor. The prefactor and the number of central copies are polynomial in $1/s$. Completing the velocity square bounds the remaining velocity factor by $Ce^{-4|v|^2}\le CH(v)$. For <a href="#cold:tube-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cold:tube-volume">[cold:tube-volume]</a>, the transverse area at scale $s_j$ is $O(s_j^2)$, and its axial length while active is $O(1+L_{s_j})$. Projection onto the torus does not increase volume. Summation gives $C\sum_j(s_j^2+s_j^{3/2})\le Cs_0^{3/2}$. ◻

</details>

All pointwise assertions above mean essential bounds. They also hold at the time sections defined by the finite path equations: free coordinates preserve measure, and Fubini’s theorem supplies a full-measure set of paths on which the inequalities can be integrated. For each fixed terminal time this gives a full-measure endpoint set. Countably many approximation indices, lattice copies, and interval choices can be handled simultaneously. This convention will also be used for the path estimates in the next section.

<a id="sec:early"></a>
### Selection and continuation on the initial time interval

The cold estimates hold without an a priori bound on the hot part. We use them first to select a hot seed whose evolution touches a prescribed cap, and then to continue every selected evolution to a fixed time $t_1$. The second step must be uniform as the cap horizon $T$ tends to zero. The bath remains weak inside the hole at very small times; away from time zero, the thinness of the jet tubes gives the additional smallness we need. All constants in this section may depend on the fixed jet parameters. They are independent of $N$, the collision cutoff, and the seed parameter, unless explicitly stated otherwise.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### The bath and the initial source

<a id="stmt-early-bath"></a>
<div id="early:bath" class="lemma">

<a id="early:bath-bound"></a>
<a id="early:hole-distance"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Bath barrier). *There are $t_1\in(0,1/32]$, $\beta'>4$, and constants $C,c>0$, independent of $\delta$, $R_\mu$, and $s_0$, such that every finite evolution of <a href="#con:colors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:colors">[con:colors]</a> satisfies, on its interval of existence, $$\label{early:bath-bound}
 0\le u(t,x,v)\le C e^{-\beta'|v|^2}
       \exp\!\left(-\frac{cD_0(x)^2}{t}\right),
 \qquad 0<t\le t_1,
 \qquad D_0(x)=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,D_{\rm hole}^c).$$ For sufficiently small $s_0$ depending on $\delta$, one also has $$\label{early:hole-distance}
 D_0(x)\ge\delta
 \qquad (0\le t\le t_1,\ x\in\mathcal O(t)).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-early-bath"><summary>Proof of stmt-early-bath</summary>

*Proof.* The distance $D_0$ is $1$-Lipschitz. Fix $4<\beta'<\beta_0$ and a positive absolute constant $c$, and consider $$B(t,x,v)=\exp(C_1t)\exp\!\left(
       -(\beta_0-C_1t)|v|^2-\frac{cD_0(x)^2}{t}\right).$$ At almost every point of a free path, $$\begin{aligned}
 D_t\log B
 &=C_1+C_1|v|^2+\frac{cD_0^2}{t^2}
                  -\frac{2cD_0}{t}\,v\cdot\nabla D_0\\
 &\ge C_1+(C_1-c)|v|^2.
\end{aligned}$$ The last inequality is completion of the square in $D_0/t$. At fixed $(t,x)$, $B$ is a Maxwellian in velocity. Energy conservation in a collision therefore gives $P(B,B)=B\nu_B$, and, as long as $\beta_0-C_1t\ge\beta'>4$ and $e^{C_1t}\le2$, $$P^b(B,B)\le P(B,B)\le C(1+|v|)B.$$ Choose $C_1$ large enough that the preceding lower bound for $D_t\log B$ dominates $C(1+|v|)$, and then choose $t_1\le1/32$ small enough to maintain both restrictions. These choices use only $\beta_0$ and the collision normalization.

The trace of $B$ along a path starting outside the hole dominates $u^0$. Indeed, if $D_0(x)=0$, then $D_0(x+tv)\le t|v|$, so the distance factor tends to one. If $D_0(x)>0$, its trace is zero, as is $u^0(x,v)$. The differential inequality can be integrated from a positive time and then passed to this trace. The positive-gain comparison used for Proposition <a href="#cold:control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cold:control">16</a>, with the nonnegative loss omitted, yields $u\le B$. This uses only absolute continuity along paths at positive times and boundedness in the $H$ norm; it does not differentiate the initial spatial mask. Since $e^{C_1t}$ is bounded, <a href="#early:bath-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:bath-bound">[early:bath-bound]</a> follows.

For $t\le1/32$, the axial interval defining $\mathcal O_i(t)$ has half-length $$a+2t+b_{\rm sp}\le\frac18+\frac1{16}+\frac1{16}=\frac14<\frac13.$$ Its transverse distance from the corresponding lifted axis is at most $s_0$. Thus every point of $\mathcal O(t)$ lies within distance $s_0$ of the segment defining the hole. Since the hole is its open $2\delta$ neighborhood, the triangle inequality gives $D_0\ge2\delta-s_0\ge\delta$ when $s_0\le\delta$. ◻

</details>

We fix $t_1$ from now on. Recall that $$\mathcal S=2P^b(c,u)+\sum_{i\ne i'}P^b(c_i,c_{i'})$$ is the source in the hot equation: it consists of collisions between different cold colors and collisions between a cold color and the bath.

<a id="stmt-early-source-estimates"></a>
<div id="early:source-estimates" class="lemma">

<a id="early:source"></a>
**Lemma 21** (Source estimates). *For sufficiently small $s_0$ depending on $\delta$, every finite evolution satisfies $$\label{early:source}
 P(c+u,H)\le\frac{C}{t+s_N}H,
 \qquad
 0\le\mathcal S\le C_{A,\delta}t^{A-1}H,
 \qquad 0<t\le t_1,$$ on its interval of existence, for every fixed $A>0$. Here $C$ is independent of $\delta$ and $R_\mu$, and the constants are uniform for all sufficiently small $s_0$. No bound on $h$ is assumed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-early-source-estimates"><summary>Proof of stmt-early-source-estimates</summary>

*Proof.* At each $(t,x)$ there is at most one main color. Denote it by $a$, taking $a=0$ if no main color is present, and put $r=c-a$. The main-color measure bound in Lemma <a href="#cold:core-amplitude" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cold:core-amplitude">17</a> and the sheet gain estimates, including Lemma <a href="#ker:parameter-sheets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ker:parameter-sheets">8</a>, give $$P(a,H)\le\frac{C}{t+s_N}H.$$ By <a href="#cold:nonmain-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cold:nonmain-bound">[cold:nonmain-bound]</a>, for each $M>0$, $$0\le r\le\rho(t)H,\qquad \rho(t)\le C_Mt^M,$$ uniformly for sufficiently small $s_0$. The bath bound gives $u\le CH$. The quadratic gain bound of Lemma <a href="#lem:hot-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hot-bound">9</a> proves the first estimate in <a href="#early:source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:source">[early:source]</a>, absorbing the bounded terms since $t+s_N\le2$.

If $a\ne0$, <a href="#early:hole-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:hole-distance">[early:hole-distance]</a> improves the bath estimate to $u\le C e^{-c\delta^2/t}H$ at this position. The ordered cross-color sum is bounded by $2P(a,r)+P(r,r)$, so positivity yields $$\mathcal S\le
 C\left(\frac{e^{-c\delta^2/t}+\rho(t)}{t+s_N}
                 +\rho(t)+\rho(t)^2\right)H.$$ The exponential is bounded by any prescribed power of $t$, with a constant depending on that power and on $\delta$. Taking $M=A$ proves the second estimate. When $a=0$, only the last two types of terms remain, and the same conclusion follows. ◻

</details>

<a id="early:hot-comparison"></a>
Set $Z(t)=\|h(t)\|_H$. Dropping the loss in the hot path equation and using <a href="#early:source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:source">[early:source]</a>, we obtain, on every interval where $Z\le1$, $$\label{early:hot-comparison}
 Z(t)\le Z(r)+\int_r^t\left(
       \frac{C_2}{s+s_N}Z(s)+C_{A,\delta}s^{A-1}\right)\,\mathrm ds,
 \qquad 0\le r\le t\le t_1.$$ The constant $C_2$ includes the quadratic hot gain, because $Z^2\le Z$ and $s+s_N\le2$. It is independent of $\delta$, $R_\mu$, $s_0$, and $N$. We will use integral inequalities such as <a href="#early:hot-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:hot-comparison">[early:hot-comparison]</a>, without assuming differentiability of the norm $Z$.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Selecting a solution that touches the cap

<a id="early:exponents"></a>
Fix the exponent in the seed <a href="#con:seed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:seed">[con:seed]</a> so that $$\label{early:exponents}
 p>C_2+1,
 \qquad A>p+C_2+3,
 \qquad m_T=T^p.$$ We call the solution with zero hot seed the *dormant solution*. The following selection adapts the cap argument of (OpenAI 2026, sec. 5) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[OpenAIPeriodic](#ref-OpenAIPeriodic)]; we give the continuity argument here for the present cutoff and color system.

<a id="stmt-early-cap-selection"></a>
<div id="early:cap-selection" class="proposition">

<a id="early:dormant-bound"></a>
**Proposition 22** (Cap selection). *For fixed $\delta>0$, there are $\bar s_0,T_0>0$, uniform in $N$ and the collision cutoff, with the following property. For each $0<s_0\le\bar s_0$, each $0<T\le T_0$, and each finite approximation, a seed parameter $\lambda\in(0,L_0)$ gives a solution on $[0,T]$ satisfying <a id="early:cap"></a>$$\label{early:cap}
 Z(t)\le m_T(t+s_N)\quad(0\le t\le T),
 \qquad
 \max_{0\le t\le T}\frac{Z(t)}{m_T(t+s_N)}=1.$$ The dormant solution exists on a fixed initial interval and satisfies $$\label{early:dormant-bound}
 Z(t)\le C'_{A,\delta}t^A.$$ The interval and constant in <a href="#early:dormant-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:dormant-bound">[early:dormant-bound]</a> are independent of $N$, the collision cutoff, and sufficiently small $s_0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-early-cap-selection"><summary>Proof of stmt-early-cap-selection</summary>

*Proof.* For the dormant solution, the function $Y(t)=C_dt^A$ is a supersolution of <a href="#early:hot-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:hot-comparison">[early:hot-comparison]</a> if $$C_d(A-C_2)\ge C_{A,\delta},$$ because $(t+s_N)^{-1}\le t^{-1}$. At each fixed $N$, comparison begins at the regular initial section $t=0$, where both functions vanish. Choosing an initial interval on which $C_dt^A<1/2$ closes the condition $Z\le1$. The cold and bath bounds are finite in the $H$ norm at each fixed approximation. Thus the continuation criterion of Proposition <a href="#con:finite-evolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con:finite-evolution">13</a> also proves existence on that interval and gives <a href="#early:dormant-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:dormant-bound">[early:dormant-bound]</a>.

Reduce $T_0$ until this interval contains $[0,T_0]$ and $C_dT_0^{A-p-1}<1$. Then, for $0<t\le T\le T_0$, $$\frac{Z(t)}{T^p(t+s_N)}
 \le\frac{C_dt^{A-1}}{T^p}
 \le C_dT^{A-p-1}<1.$$ At $t=0$ the ratio is zero. Hence the dormant solution stays strictly below the cap. At the other endpoint, $\lambda=L_0$, the ratio at time zero equals $L_0\|\chi\|_H>1$.

Fix $T,N$, and the cutoff. Let $I$ be the set of $\lambda\in[0,L_0]$ whose solutions exist on $[0,T]$ and remain strictly below the cap there. Continuous dependence in the local theory and continuation at the terminal section make $I$ relatively open. It contains $0$ and does not contain $L_0$. Let $\lambda_*$ be the right endpoint of the connected component of $I$ containing zero. Along a sequence $\lambda_k\uparrow\lambda_*$ within that component, all hot parts are bounded by the cap. The cold and bath estimates bound the remaining colors uniformly in $k$ in the fixed-approximation norm. The difference estimate in Proposition <a href="#con:finite-evolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con:finite-evolution">13</a> therefore makes these solutions Cauchy, uniformly on $[0,T]$ in free coordinates. Their limit is the solution with parameter $\lambda_*$ and satisfies the non-strict cap inequality.

The norm $Z$ is continuous in time, and the cap denominator is positive, so the displayed ratio has a maximum. If that maximum were less than one, continuous dependence and continuation would place a neighborhood of $\lambda_*$ in $I$, contrary to its endpoint property. Its maximum is therefore one. Since $0$ has a neighborhood in $I$, while $L_0\|\chi\|_H>1$ also excludes a neighborhood of $L_0$, one has $0<\lambda_*<L_0$. A final reduction of $T_0$ makes the cap at most one on $[0,T]$ for every $T\le T_0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Smallness through the fixed bath time

The pointwise estimate in <a href="#early:source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:source">[early:source]</a> is very strong near zero, but its constant depends on the hole width. To reach the fixed time $t_1$ with an arbitrarily small hot part, we also use the shrinking spatial support of the main colors.

<a id="stmt-early-path-source"></a>
<div id="early:path-source" class="lemma">

<a id="early:source-integral"></a>
**Lemma 23** (Small source along free paths). *Fix $0<t_b<t_1$. There is a function $E_{t_b}(s_0)$ tending to zero as $s_0\to0$ such that, for every finite evolution and every terminal time $t\in[t_b,t_1]$ in its interval of existence, $$\label{early:source-integral}
 \left\|\int_{t_b}^t
    \mathcal S(s,x-(t-s)v,v)\,\mathrm ds\right\|_H
 \le E_{t_b}(s_0).$$ This estimate is uniform in the hot seed, the cap horizon, the approximation index, and the collision cutoff.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-early-path-source"><summary>Proof of stmt-early-path-source</summary>

*Proof.* On $[t_b,t_1]$, the nonmain bound <a href="#cold:nonmain-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cold:nonmain-bound">[cold:nonmain-bound]</a> gives $r\le\varepsilon(s_0)H$, with $\varepsilon(s_0)\to0$. The main-color sheet amplitude is bounded by $C/t_b$. Consequently all terms of $\mathcal S$ containing a nonmain factor are bounded by $C_{t_b}\varepsilon(s_0)H$, after enlarging $\varepsilon$ if needed.

<a id="early:common-majorant"></a>
For the remaining main–bath term, define a deterministic majorant $$\mathcal U_{\mathrm{main}}(s,x,v)
   =\sum_i\mathbf 1_{\mathcal O_i(s)}(x)\mathcal U_i(s,x,v),
 \qquad U_{\mathrm{bath}}(v)=C e^{-\beta'|v|^2},$$ $$\mathcal S_{\mathrm{mb}}(s,x,v)
   =2P(\mathcal U_{\mathrm{main}}(s,x,\cdot),U_{\mathrm{bath}})(v).$$ Here the sum includes all scales, so it bounds every finite selection; at each position it has at most one nonzero summand. By Proposition <a href="#cold:control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cold:control">16</a> and <a href="#early:bath-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:bath-bound">[early:bath-bound]</a>, $$\label{early:common-majorant}
 0\le\mathcal S(s,x,v)
 \le C_{t_b}\varepsilon(s_0)H(v)+\mathcal S_{\mathrm{mb}}(s,x,v),
 \qquad t_b\le s\le t_1.$$ The sheet gain estimates give $\mathcal S_{\mathrm{mb}}\le C_{t_b}H$. The uniform large-output estimate <a href="#ker:large-output" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:large-output">[ker:large-output]</a> gives <a id="early:output-tail"></a>$$\label{early:output-tail}
 \sup_{s,x,\,|v|>R}\frac{\mathcal S_{\mathrm{mb}}(s,x,v)}{H(v)}
 \le\omega_{t_b}(R),\qquad \omega_{t_b}(R)\longrightarrow0
 \quad(R\to\infty),$$ with essential suprema in the velocity variable. To handle velocities near the axis, fix $q'\in(1,q)$ and write $$H_{q'}(v)=e^{-|v|^2}\langle v\rangle^{-100}
                 (1+|\bar v|^{-q'}).$$ Since $U_{\mathrm{bath}}\le C_{q'}H_{q'}$, the same sheet lemma with exponent $q'$ implies $\mathcal S_{\mathrm{mb}}\le C_{t_b,q'}H_{q'}$. Hence, for $0<r\le1$, <a id="early:output-axis"></a>$$\label{early:output-axis}
 \sup_{s,x,\,0<|\bar v|<r}\frac{\mathcal S_{\mathrm{mb}}(s,x,v)}{H(v)}
 \le C_{t_b,q'}r^{q-q'}.$$ Both estimates include the Gaussian-parameter measures in Lemma <a href="#cold:core-amplitude" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cold:core-amplitude">17</a>, by Lemma <a href="#ker:parameter-sheets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ker:parameter-sheets">8</a>.

It remains to consider the compact velocity region $|v|\le R$, $|\bar v|\ge r$. The spatial support of $\mathcal S_{\mathrm{mb}}$ is contained, for every $s\le t_1$, in the projection of the fixed union $$\bigcup_{n\in\Lambda}
     \bigl(n+B^2_{s_0}\times[-1/4,1/4]\bigr),$$ where $B^2_{s_0}$ is the transverse disk of radius $s_0$. A lifted free path with $|v|\le R$ and duration at most $t_1$ can meet only $C_R$ of these cylinders, uniformly in its endpoint: their centers must lie in a fixed bounded enlargement of a segment of length at most $Rt_1$, and the lattice has a fixed separation. For any one cylinder, its transverse projection is crossed in time at most $2s_0/|\bar v|\le2s_0/r$. Thus the normalized integral of $\mathcal S_{\mathrm{mb}}$ along such a path is bounded by $C_{t_b,R}s_0/r$. Combining the three velocity regions gives $$\left\|\int_{t_b}^t
     \mathcal S(s,x-(t-s)v,v)\,\mathrm ds\right\|_H
 \le C_{t_b}\varepsilon(s_0)
      +t_1\omega_{t_b}(R)
      +C_{t_b,q'}t_1r^{q-q'}
      +C_{t_b,R}\frac{s_0}{r}.$$ First take $R$ large, then $r$ small, and finally $s_0$ small. This proves <a href="#early:source-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:source-integral">[early:source-integral]</a>.

All estimates above concern the common pointwise majorant <a href="#early:common-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:common-majorant">[early:common-majorant]</a>, rather than values chosen separately on individual paths. To pass an almost-everywhere inequality to path integrals, use free coordinates $(s,x_0,v)\mapsto(s,x_0+sv,v)$. This map preserves measure, so Fubini’s theorem gives the inequality at almost every time on almost every free path. Integration on those paths then defines representatives at every terminal section. Since free transport preserves $\|\cdot\|_H$, the resulting bound is precisely the essential norm in <a href="#early:source-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:source-integral">[early:source-integral]</a> at each fixed terminal time. The construction applies on any existing solution interval, so this lemma does not presuppose continuation to $t_1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-early-continuation"></a>
<div id="early:continuation" class="proposition">

<a id="early:small-hot"></a>
**Proposition 24** (Uniform early continuation). *Fix $\delta>0$ and $\zeta\in(0,1)$. There are $\bar s_0>0$ and $T_0\in(0,t_1)$, depending on $\delta$ and $\zeta$ and on the fixed jet parameters, such that for every $0<s_0\le\bar s_0$, every finite approximation, and every $0<T\le T_0$, the dormant solution and every solution selected in Proposition <a href="#early:cap-selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="early:cap-selection">22</a> continue to $t_1$ and satisfy $$\label{early:small-hot}
 \sup_{0\le t\le t_1}\|h(t)\|_H\le\zeta.$$ The choices are uniform in $N$, the collision cutoff, $R_\mu$, and $T$. For dormant solutions, <a href="#early:dormant-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:dormant-bound">[early:dormant-bound]</a> also holds on an initial interval independent of these indices, with the fixed exponent $A>p+1$ chosen in <a href="#early:exponents" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:exponents">[early:exponents]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-early-continuation"><summary>Proof of stmt-early-continuation</summary>

<a id="early:intermediate-bound"></a>
*Proof.* We choose an intermediate time $t_b\in(0,t_1)$ and then require $T_0<t_b$. At the cap horizon, both the selected and dormant solutions satisfy $$Z(T)\le T^p(T+s_N)\le2T^p,$$ using only $T,s_N\le1$; no relation between $T$ and $s_N$ is needed. For $T\le t\le t_b$, <a href="#early:hot-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:hot-comparison">[early:hot-comparison]</a> and $(s+s_N)^{-1}\le s^{-1}$ give, while $Z\le1$, $$\label{early:intermediate-bound}
 Z(t)\le2T^{p-C_2}t^{C_2}
                  +\widetilde C_{A,\delta}t^A.$$ Indeed multiplication of the scalar comparison by $t^{-C_2}$ integrates the source to at most $C_{A,\delta}t^{A-C_2}/(A-C_2)$.

<a id="early:final-comparison"></a>
From $t_b$ onward, retain the source inside the path integral rather than using its pointwise bound. The same hot gain estimates and Lemma <a href="#early:path-source" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="early:path-source">23</a> give $$Z(t)\le Z(t_b)+E_{t_b}(s_0)
                 +\int_{t_b}^t\frac{C_2}{s}Z(s)\,\mathrm ds.$$ Consequently, as long as $Z\le1$, $$\label{early:final-comparison}
 \sup_{t_b\le t\le t_1}Z(t)
 \le\left(\frac{t_1}{t_b}\right)^{C_2}
       \left(2T_0^{p-C_2}t_b^{C_2}
              +\widetilde C_{A,\delta}t_b^A+E_{t_b}(s_0)\right),$$ where the supremum is restricted to the current interval of existence until continuation has been established.

The order of choices now matters. Since $A>C_2$, choose $t_b$ so that $$\widetilde C_{A,\delta}t_1^{C_2}t_b^{A-C_2}<\frac\zeta6.$$ Since $p>C_2$, reduce $T_0<t_b$, within the range of Proposition <a href="#early:cap-selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="early:cap-selection">22</a>, so that $$2T_0^{p-C_2}t_1^{C_2}<\frac\zeta6,
 \qquad 2T_0^p<\frac\zeta2.$$ Finally take $\bar s_0$ small enough for all preceding cold and bath estimates and for $$\left(\frac{t_1}{t_b}\right)^{C_2}E_{t_b}(s_0)<\frac\zeta6
 \qquad(0<s_0\le\bar s_0).$$ The last choice is possible by Lemma <a href="#early:path-source" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="early:path-source">23</a>. Equation <a href="#early:final-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:final-comparison">[early:final-comparison]</a> is then strictly below $\zeta/2$. The same first two choices make <a href="#early:intermediate-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:intermediate-bound">[early:intermediate-bound]</a> strictly below $\zeta/2$ on $[T,t_b]$, and the cap gives the same bound on $[0,T]$.

These estimates close the condition $Z\le1$ with a strict margin. At each fixed approximation the cold sum and bath have bounded $H$ norm through $t_1$, so the finite-evolution continuation criterion excludes a finite endpoint before $t_1$. This proves existence and <a href="#early:small-hot" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="early:small-hot">[early:small-hot]</a> uniformly in all the stated indices. The dormant estimate was obtained independently on its initial interval and is preserved under this continuation. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:global"></a>
### A uniform global bound for the finite evolutions

The estimates before $t_1$ give both families of finite evolutions a common starting point for continuation. We now prove that they exist for all time, with a uniform bound on the part of the density that contains the bath, the hot component, and the retired jets. The characteristic comparisons retain the color decomposition used in (OpenAI 2026, secs. 5–6) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[OpenAIPeriodic](#ref-OpenAIPeriodic)]. The additional control comes from the bath: the resulting small entropy distance of $F$ from $\mu$ supplies a positive collision frequency and makes the nonlinear gain uniformly bounded after averaging along free flights. Entropy smallness and characteristic estimates also underlie the bounded-amplitude theory of (Duan et al. 2017, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[DuanHuangWangYang2017](#ref-DuanHuangWangYang2017)]. Here the cold components remain separate until retirement, while Lemma <a href="#lem:gain-small-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gain-small-mass">10</a> controls the gain using a majorant singular on the velocity axis.

<a id="global:w-equation"></a>
Recall from <a href="#con:late-colors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:late-colors">[con:late-colors]</a> that, for $t\ge t_1$, <a id="global:decomposition"></a>$$\label{global:decomposition}
 F=c+w,\qquad c=\sum_{i\text{ active}}c_i,
 \qquad w(t_1)=u(t_1)+h(t_1).$$ Between retirement times the equation for $w$ is $$\label{global:w-equation}
 (D_t+\nu_F^b)w=P^b(w,w)+2P^b(c,w)+\mathcal S_c,
 \qquad
 \mathcal S_c=\sum_{i\ne i',\ i,i'\text{ active}}P^b(c_i,c_{i'}).$$ The sum is ordered. At $L_s=s^{-1/2}$ we add the jets of scale $s$ to $w$ and remove them from $c$, so that $F$ has no jump. All bounds below hold for the characteristic representatives, at each time section and almost everywhere in $(x,v)$, as in the finite evolution construction.

<a id="global:initial-bound"></a>
<a id="global:bootstrap"></a>
The bath bound in <a href="#early:bath" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="early:bath">20</a> and <a href="#early:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="early:continuation">24</a>, with $\zeta<1$, give a constant $B_0$ such that $$\label{global:initial-bound}
 \|u(t)+h(t)\|_H\le B_0\quad(0\le t\le t_1).$$ Here $B_0$ is independent of the hole, the bath cutoff, the smallest scale, and the approximation. Fix provisionally $B>B_0+2$ and consider an interval of existence beginning at $t_1$ on which $$\label{global:bootstrap}
 0\le w(t,x,v)\le B H(v).$$ We will improve this bound by a constant independent of $B$, after making the entropy error and the remaining small parameters sufficiently small depending on $B$.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Averaging and a positive collision frequency

The spatial entropy estimate is an $L^1_x$ bound, whereas our continuation argument needs estimates at almost every spatial point. Velocity integration along flights makes this passage possible. We first record the elementary averaging estimate in a form that also applies over arbitrarily long time intervals. Write $M_H=\int H(v)\,\mathrm dv<\infty$.

<a id="stmt-global-flight-average"></a>
<div id="global:flight-average" class="lemma">

<a id="global:ball-average"></a>
<a id="global:damped-average"></a>
<a id="global:early-average"></a>
**Lemma 25** (Averaging along free flights). *Let $A:[0,\infty)\times(\mathbb R^3/\Lambda)\to[0,\infty)$ be measurable, write $A_s=A(s,\cdot)$, and suppose $$\sup_s\|A_s\|_\infty\le A_*,\qquad
 \sup_s\|A_s\|_1\le a_*.$$ For a fixed bounded velocity set $V$ and $\rho>0$, $$\label{global:ball-average}
 \int_V A_s(x-\tau v)\,\mathrm dv
 \le C_{V,\rho}a_*
 \qquad(\tau\ge\rho),$$ uniformly in $x,s,\tau$. If $A_*$ is fixed, the following quantities tend to zero as $a_*\to0$: $$\begin{aligned}
 &\sup_{t\ge t_1,x}
 \int_{t_1}^t e^{-\kappa(t-s)}
       \int H(v)A_s(x-(t-s)v)\,\mathrm dv\,\mathrm ds,
       \label{global:damped-average}\\
 &\sup_{t\ge t_1,x}
 \int_0^{t_1}\int H(v)A_s(x-(t-s)v)\,\mathrm dv\,\mathrm ds,
       \label{global:early-average}
\end{aligned}$$ where $\kappa>0$ and $t_1>0$ are fixed. The supremum in $x$ is essential.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-global-flight-average"><summary>Proof of stmt-global-flight-average</summary>

*Proof.* Contain $V$ in a ball of radius $R$. The change of variables $z=x-\tau v$ gives a factor $\tau^{-3}$. The image ball meets at most $C(1+\tau R)^3$ translates of a fixed bounded fundamental cell of $\Lambda$. Periodicity therefore gives $$\int_V A_s(x-\tau v)\,\mathrm dv
 \le C\tau^{-3}(1+\tau R)^3\|A_s\|_1,$$ which proves <a href="#global:ball-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:ball-average">[global:ball-average]</a>. In particular, its constant does not grow when $\tau$ tends to infinity.

For $L\ge1$ let $$H_L(v)=\min\{H(v),L\}\mathbf 1_{\{|v|\le L\}},\qquad
 \delta_L=\int(H-H_L)\,\mathrm dv.$$ Then $\delta_L\to0$, and <a href="#global:ball-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:ball-average">[global:ball-average]</a> implies, for $\tau\ge\rho$, <a id="global:weighted-average"></a>$$\label{global:weighted-average}
 \int H(v)A_s(x-\tau v)\,\mathrm dv
 \le A_*\delta_L+C_{L,\rho}a_*.$$ For $0\le\tau<\rho$, the same integral is at most $A_*M_H$. Consequently the expression in <a href="#global:damped-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:damped-average">[global:damped-average]</a> is at most $$\rho A_*M_H+\kappa^{-1}
       (A_*\delta_L+C_{L,\rho}a_*),$$ and that in <a href="#global:early-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:early-average">[global:early-average]</a> is at most $$\rho A_*M_H+t_1(A_*\delta_L+C_{L,\rho}a_*).$$ Choose first $\rho$ small, then $L$ large, and finally $a_*$ small. Both upper bounds tend to zero in this order. The same proof works when the time integrals are restricted to an interval of existence. ◻

</details>

<a id="global:entropy-distance"></a>
<a id="global:moment-bound"></a>
Recall the entropy quantities from <a href="#con:entropy-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:entropy-distance">[con:entropy-distance]</a>: $$\label{global:entropy-distance}
 \begin{aligned}
 D_F(t,x)&=\int\langle v\rangle|F(t,x,v)-\mu(v)|\,\mathrm dv,
 &\|D_F(t)\|_1&\le\varepsilon_E,\\
 D_-(t,x)&=\int\langle v\rangle(\mu(v)-F(t,x,v))_+\,\mathrm dv.
 \end{aligned}$$ The second quantity is bounded pointwise by $\int\langle v\rangle\mu(v)\,\mathrm dv$, independently of the bootstrap. The first has a pointwise bound $$\label{global:moment-bound}
 D_F(t,x)\le C(B),\qquad t\ge t_1/2,$$ as long as <a href="#global:bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:bootstrap">[global:bootstrap]</a> holds after $t_1$. Indeed, before $t_1$ use <a href="#global:initial-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:initial-bound">[global:initial-bound]</a>; after $t_1$ use $w\le BH$. At every point there is at most one active main jet, whose mass and first moment are bounded by $C/(t+s)\le2C/t_1$ by <a href="#cold:core-amplitude" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cold:core-amplitude">17</a>. The other jets are controlled by <a href="#cold:nonmain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cold:nonmain">18</a>. These bounds prove <a href="#global:moment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:moment-bound">[global:moment-bound]</a> uniformly in the number of retained scales.

<a id="stmt-global-frequency"></a>
<div id="global:frequency" class="lemma">

<a id="global:frequency-bound"></a>
**Lemma 26** (A collision frequency independent of the bootstrap size). *There is a constant $\kappa>0$, depending only on the fixed Maxwellian $\mu$ and $t_1$, with the following property. For each fixed $B>B_0+2$, if $\varepsilon_E$ is sufficiently small depending on $B$, then every finite evolution satisfying <a href="#global:bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:bootstrap">[global:bootstrap]</a> obeys $$\label{global:frequency-bound}
 \nu_F^b(t,x,v)\ge\kappa\qquad(t\ge t_1).$$ The constant is uniform for $b\ge1$ and for all approximation indices.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-global-frequency"><summary>Proof of stmt-global-frequency</summary>

<a id="global:gain-lower"></a>
*Proof.* Fix two closed velocity balls $V_1,V_2$ of positive radius with $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(V_1,V_2)>3$, and put $V=V_1\cup V_2$. All constants until the choice of the entropy tolerance may depend on these fixed balls. Since $b\ge1$ and $\mu$ is a Maxwellian, $$P^b(\mu,\mu)(v)=\mu(v)\nu_\mu^b(v)
 \ge \mu(v)\nu_\mu^1(v)\ge m_0>0
 \qquad(v\in V).$$ At a fixed $(t,x)$ write $d=(\mu-F)_+$. Positivity of the gain and $F\ge\mu-d$ imply $$P^b(F,F)\ge P^b(\mu-d,\mu-d)
 \ge P^b(\mu,\mu)-2P(\mu,d).$$ By the plane formula <a href="#ker:carleman" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ker:carleman">[ker:carleman]</a>, the Gaussian integral over each plane is bounded, so $$P(\mu,d)(v)\le C\int\frac{d(p)}{|p-v|}\,\mathrm dp.$$ For $|p-v|<r$, use $d\le\mu\le1$ to bound this integral by $Cr^2$; for $|p-v|\ge r$, it is at most $Cr^{-1}D_F(t,x)$. Choose $r>0$ once so that the first contribution to $2P(\mu,d)$ is at most $m_0/2$. With $m_*=m_0/2$ and a fixed constant $C$, we obtain $$\label{global:gain-lower}
 P^b(F,F)(t,x,v)\ge m_*-CD_F(t,x),\qquad
 \nu_F^b(t,x,v)\le C+CD_F(t,x),\qquad v\in V.$$ The second inequality follows directly from $\min\{b,|v-p|\}\le |v-p|\le C\langle p\rangle$ on $V$.

<a id="global:survival-lower"></a>
Let $\Delta=t_1/2$, fix $t\ge t_1$, and write $X_\tau=x-(t-\tau)v$. The Duhamel formula for the total density, whose value is continuous through retirements, gives $$F(t,x,v)\ge
 \int_{t-\Delta}^{t-\Delta/2}
 e^{-\int_s^t\nu_F^b(\tau,X_\tau,v)\,\mathrm d\tau}
 P^b(F,F)(s,X_s,v)\,\mathrm ds.$$ Using <a href="#global:gain-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:gain-lower">[global:gain-lower]</a>, $e^{-a}\ge1-a$ for $a\ge0$, and the upper bound $1$ for a survival factor, the integrand is bounded below by $$\label{global:survival-lower}
 m_*e^{-C\Delta}
 \left(1-C\int_s^t D_F(\tau,X_\tau)\,\mathrm d\tau\right)
 -CD_F(s,X_s).$$ This inequality remains valid when its right side is negative.

Integrate <a href="#global:survival-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:survival-lower">[global:survival-lower]</a> over $s$ and over either ball $V_k$. The positive constant contributes $$a_k:=|V_k|(\Delta/2)m_*e^{-C\Delta}>0.$$ Choose $0<\rho<\Delta/2$. For $t-\tau\ge\rho$, <a href="#global:ball-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:ball-average">[global:ball-average]</a> and <a href="#global:entropy-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:entropy-distance">[global:entropy-distance]</a> give <a id="global:entropy-flight"></a>$$\label{global:entropy-flight}
 \int_{V_k}D_F(\tau,x-(t-\tau)v)\,\mathrm dv
 \le C_{V_k,\rho}\varepsilon_E.$$ The term $D_F(s,X_s)$ always has $t-s\ge\Delta/2$. Its integrated cost is therefore $C_\rho\varepsilon_E$. For the double time integral in <a href="#global:survival-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:survival-lower">[global:survival-lower]</a>, the portion with $t-\tau\ge\rho$ has the same type of bound, whereas the remaining portion costs at most $C(B)\rho$ by <a href="#global:moment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:moment-bound">[global:moment-bound]</a>. Thus $$\int_{V_k}F(t,x,v)\,\mathrm dv
 \ge a_k-C_\rho\varepsilon_E-C(B)\rho.$$ After $B$ has been fixed, choose $\rho$ to make the last term at most $\min(a_1,a_2)/4$, and then choose $\varepsilon_E$ to make the middle term at most the same quantity. It follows that $$\int_{V_k}F(t,x,v)\,\mathrm dv\ge a_k/2\qquad(k=1,2).$$ For any velocity $v$, one of the balls has distance at least $1$ from $v$: otherwise their mutual distance would be less than $2$. On that ball $\min\{b,|v-p|\}\ge1$. Consequently $$\nu_F^b(t,x,v)
 =2\pi\int\min\{b,|v-p|\}F(t,x,p)\,\mathrm dp
 \ge\pi\min(a_1,a_2)=:\kappa.$$ Only the entropy tolerance, and not this value of $\kappa$, depended on $B$. ◻

</details>

<a id="global:cold-decay"></a>
<a id="global:cold-gain"></a>
<a id="global:cold-source"></a>
The positive frequency gives exponential decay to every active jet. Specifically, <a href="#cold:decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cold:decay">[cold:decay]</a> yields $$\label{global:cold-decay}
 c_i(t,x,v)\le e^{-\kappa(t-t_1)}\mathcal U_i(t,x,v),
 \qquad t_1\le t\le L_{s_i}.$$ At a point in the active tube union $\mathcal O(t)$ there is at most one main color. Its sheet amplitude is at most $C/t_1$; outside the union all colors are nonmain. Applying <a href="#lem:sheet-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sheet-bound">6</a> and <a href="#cold:nonmain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cold:nonmain">18</a>, and enlarging its small coefficient if necessary, gives constants $C_3,C_4$ independent of $B$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 P(c,H)&\le C_3e^{-\kappa(t-t_1)}H
            \bigl(\mathbf 1_{\mathcal O(t)}+\theta_0\bigr),
              \label{global:cold-gain}\\
 \mathcal S_c&\le C_4\theta_0H,
              \label{global:cold-source}
\end{aligned}$$ where $\theta_0=\theta_0(s_0)\to0$ as $s_0\to0$. For the second estimate, if $c_*$ is the main color and $r$ the sum of the remaining colors, the ordered cross sum is at most $2P(c_*,r)+P(r,r)$. Here $r\le\theta_0e^{-4|v|^2}$ and the sheet bound controls $P(c_*,H)$; if there is no main color use $c\le
\theta_0e^{-4|v|^2}$ and <a href="#lem:hot-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hot-bound">9</a>.

<a id="global:retirement"></a>
At a retirement time, <a href="#cold:retirement-size" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cold:retirement-size">19</a> and <a href="#global:cold-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:cold-decay">[global:cold-decay]</a> bound the increment of $w$ by $J_sH$, where $$\label{global:retirement}
 J_s=C s^{-C}e^{-\kappa(L_s-t_1)},\qquad
 \sum_sJ_s\le J_0(s_0):=
 \sum_{j\ge1}C s_j^{-C}e^{-\kappa(s_j^{-1/2}-t_1)}\longrightarrow0.$$ This follows for the one-sided trace just before retirement, using only the bootstrap up to that time. To check the last limit explicitly, the exponential dominates every power of $s$: for sufficiently small $s$, $Cs^{-C}e^{-\kappa(s^{-1/2}-t_1)}\le s^2$. The remaining geometric sum is at most $s_0^2/(e^{2\eta}-1)$. In particular the sum of all retirement increments can be made small uniformly in $N$.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Small excess mass and a bounded nonlinear gain

The remaining obstruction is the quadratic term $P^b(w,w)$: estimating it directly from $w\le BH$ would produce a constant of order $B^2$. Instead we compare $w$ with the fixed Maxwellian. Entropy bounds the spatial integral of its positive excess, and the preceding flight estimate converts that bound into small excess mass at each position. The small-mass gain estimate then controls the quadratic term by a constant chosen before $B$.

<a id="stmt-global-gain"></a>
<div id="global:gain" class="lemma">

<a id="global:gain-bound"></a>
**Lemma 27** (Uniform nonlinear gain). *Let $B>B_0+2$ be fixed and suppose <a href="#global:bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:bootstrap">[global:bootstrap]</a> holds on an interval beginning at $t_1$. After taking $\varepsilon_E$, $\zeta$, and $s_0$ sufficiently small depending on $B$, one has on that interval $$\label{global:gain-bound}
 P^b(w,w)\le C_5H,
 \qquad C_5:=\|P(\mu,\mu)\|_H+1.$$ In particular, $C_5$ is independent of $B$, the approximation, and the cap horizon. The estimate holds also at the appropriate one-sided sections of retirement times.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-global-gain"><summary>Proof of stmt-global-gain</summary>

<a id="global:gain-difference"></a>
<a id="global:loss-difference"></a>
*Proof.* Choose the entropy tolerance small enough for <a href="#global:frequency" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="global:frequency">26</a>, so that the damping constant $\kappa$ and <a href="#global:cold-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:cold-gain">[global:cold-gain]</a>–<a href="#global:retirement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:retirement">[global:retirement]</a> are available. Define <a id="global:excess"></a>$$\label{global:excess}
 j=(w-\mu)_+,\qquad m_j(t,x)=\int j(t,x,v)\,\mathrm dv.$$ Since $w\le F$ and $j\le BH$, <a id="global:excess-mass"></a>$$\label{global:excess-mass}
 0\le m_j\le BM_H,
 \qquad\|m_j(t)\|_1\le\varepsilon_E.$$ Write $C_\mu=\|\mu\|_H<\infty$ and $A_B=2C_\mu+B$. The inequality $w\le\mu+j$, bilinearity, and positivity give $$\label{global:gain-difference}
 P^b(w,w)-P^b(\mu,\mu)
 \le2P(\mu,j)+P(j,j)\le A_BP(H,j).$$ The frequency deficit has the bound $$\label{global:loss-difference}
 (\nu_\mu^b-\nu_F^b)\mu
 \le2\pi\mu(v)\int|v-p|(\mu(p)-F(p))_+\,\mathrm dp
 \le CH(v)D_-.$$ Here $\mu(v)\langle v\rangle\le CH(v)$.

<a id="global:excess-source"></a>
Between retirements, subtracting the stationary Maxwellian equation from <a href="#global:w-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:w-equation">[global:w-equation]</a> gives $$(D_t+\nu_F^b)(w-\mu)
 =P^b(w,w)-P^b(\mu,\mu)
   +(\nu_\mu^b-\nu_F^b)\mu+2P^b(c,w)+\mathcal S_c.$$ The chain rule for the positive part on almost every free flight therefore gives an upper comparison for $j$ with damping $\kappa$. Using <a href="#lem:gain-small-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gain-small-mass">10</a> in <a href="#global:gain-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:gain-difference">[global:gain-difference]</a>, and then <a href="#global:loss-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:loss-difference">[global:loss-difference]</a>, <a href="#global:cold-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:cold-gain">[global:cold-gain]</a>, and <a href="#global:cold-source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:cold-source">[global:cold-source]</a>, shows that for each $\epsilon>0$ its positive source is at most $H(v)R(s,x)$, where $$\label{global:excess-source}
 R(s,x)=\epsilon+C_{B,\epsilon}m_j(s,x)+CD_-(s,x)
           +C(B)\bigl(\mathbf 1_{\mathcal O(s)}(x)+\theta_0\bigr).$$ Constants have been enlarged to absorb $A_B$. At a retirement time, the increase of $j$ is at most the increase of $w$, because $(a+d)_+-a_+\le d$ for $d\ge0$. Thus its increments are bounded by the same $J_sH$ as in <a href="#global:retirement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:retirement">[global:retirement]</a>.

<a id="global:bath-excess-source"></a>
We also need to estimate the excess already present in the bath at $t_1$. Put $j_u=(u-\mu)_+$ and $m_{j_u}=\int j_u\,\mathrm dv$ for $0\le s\le t_1$. The bath starts below $\mu$, so $j_u(0)=0$; <a href="#early:bath" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="early:bath">20</a> gives the fixed bound $j_u\le B_0H$. Since $u\le F$, <a id="global:bath-excess-mass"></a>$$\label{global:bath-excess-mass}
 \|m_{j_u}(s)\|_\infty\le B_0M_H,
 \qquad\|m_{j_u}(s)\|_1\le\varepsilon_E.$$ The bath equation is $(D_t+\nu_F^b)u=P^b(u,u)$. Subtracting the Maxwellian equation and applying the same two difference estimates as above gives, now discarding the nonnegative damping, a source bounded by $H(v)R_u(s,x)$, where $$\label{global:bath-excess-source}
 R_u(s,x)=\epsilon+C_{B_0,\epsilon}m_{j_u}(s,x)+CD_-(s,x).$$ The cold density appears only through the frequency deficit, which is controlled by $D_-$. No positive source involving a cold gain occurs in this bath equation.

At $t_1$, the hot bound gives $j(t_1)\le j_u(t_1)+\zeta H$. Continue the same backward free flight through $t_1$, and write $X_s=x-(t-s)v$. The two comparisons, together with all intervening retirements, imply <a id="global:excess-path"></a>$$\label{global:excess-path}
 \frac{j(t,x,v)}{H(v)}
 \le\zeta+J_0
   +\int_0^{t_1}R_u(s,X_s)\,\mathrm ds
   +\int_{t_1}^t e^{-\kappa(t-s)}R(s,X_s)\,\mathrm ds.$$ We have dropped damping from the initial and retirement contributions; doing so also allows the bath comparison to run along that one flight all the way back to zero.

<a id="global:constant-errors"></a>
<a id="global:small-excess"></a>
We claim that the velocity integral of the right side times $H$ can be made smaller than any prescribed number $\tau>0$, uniformly in $(t,x)$ on the bootstrap interval. The constant terms contribute at most $$\label{global:constant-errors}
 M_H\left(\zeta+J_0+\epsilon(t_1+\kappa^{-1})
                      +\kappa^{-1}C(B)\theta_0\right).$$ For the remaining terms, all the spatial functions in question have a fixed pointwise upper bound once $B$ is fixed: $m_j\le BM_H$, $m_{j_u}\le B_0M_H$, $D_-\le\int\langle v\rangle\mu\,\mathrm dv$, and $\mathbf 1_{\mathcal O(s)}\le1$. Their spatial integrals are respectively bounded by $\varepsilon_E$, $\varepsilon_E$, $\varepsilon_E$, and $Cs_0^{3/2}$, the last by <a href="#cold:tube-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cold:tube-volume">[cold:tube-volume]</a>. Apply <a href="#global:flight-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="global:flight-average">25</a> to each term, with its coefficient in <a href="#global:excess-source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:excess-source">[global:excess-source]</a> or <a href="#global:bath-excess-source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:bath-excess-source">[global:bath-excess-source]</a>. For clarity, the choices are made in the following order. First choose $\epsilon$ so that its contribution to <a href="#global:constant-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:constant-errors">[global:constant-errors]</a> is less than $\tau/4$; all the coefficients $C_{B,\epsilon}$ and $C_{B_0,\epsilon}$ are then fixed. For each of the finitely many averaged terms, choose the time deletion length $\rho$ and the truncation $H_L$ in the proof of that lemma so that the endpoint and velocity-tail errors, including its coefficient, have total less than $\tau/4$. Next make $\varepsilon_E$ and $s_0$ sufficiently small for the remaining averaged errors to total less than $\tau/4$. Finally take $\zeta$ small and, if necessary, reduce $s_0$ further to make the other terms in <a href="#global:constant-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:constant-errors">[global:constant-errors]</a> less than $\tau/4$. All these choices are uniform in the endpoint time. Thus $$\label{global:small-excess}
 m_j(t,x)\le\tau.$$ The same calculation on a pre-retirement section includes only the jumps that have already occurred, so it does not require a post-jump bootstrap hypothesis.

It remains to select the particular smallness threshold that controls the gain. Apply <a href="#lem:gain-small-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gain-small-mass">10</a> once more, now with $\epsilon'=1/(2A_B)$. Write its constant as $C_{B,\epsilon'}$ and set $$\tau=\frac{1}{2A_B\max\{1,C_{B,\epsilon'}\}}.$$ Choose the parameters in the preceding argument to give <a href="#global:small-excess" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:small-excess">[global:small-excess]</a> for this $\tau$. Then $$A_BP(H,j)\le
 A_BH\bigl(\epsilon'+C_{B,\epsilon'}m_j\bigr)\le H.$$ Equation <a href="#global:gain-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:gain-difference">[global:gain-difference]</a> and $P^b(\mu,\mu)\le
P(\mu,\mu)$ prove <a href="#global:gain-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:gain-bound">[global:gain-bound]</a>. The latter has finite $H$ norm because $P(\mu,\mu)=\mu\nu_\mu$ is a Gaussian times a function of at most linear growth. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Parameter choice and continuation

We can now choose the bootstrap size and then realize all the smallness requirements imposed above. This order is essential: both the damping rate and the ultimate gain bound have already been fixed independently of the provisional value $B$.

<a id="stmt-prop-global-control"></a>
<div id="prop:global-control" class="proposition">

<a id="global:uniform-bound"></a>
**Proposition 28** (Uniform global continuation). *The construction parameters can be fixed so that every dormant finite evolution and every cap solution with $0<T\le T_0$ continues globally. There are constants $B<\infty$ and $\kappa>0$, independent of $N$, $b_N\ge1$, and $T$, such that $$\label{global:uniform-bound}
 \|w(t)\|_H\le B,\qquad \nu_F^{b_N}(t,x,v)\ge\kappa,
 \qquad t\ge t_1.$$ Every active jet satisfies <a href="#global:cold-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:cold-decay">[global:cold-decay]</a> through its retirement, the total retirement increment is bounded by <a href="#global:retirement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:retirement">[global:retirement]</a>, and the hot component satisfies the small bound of <a href="#early:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="early:continuation">24</a> on $[0,t_1]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-global-control"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-global-control</summary>

<a id="global:strict-improvement"></a>
*Proof.* On a bootstrap interval let $Z_w(t)=\|w(t)\|_H$. The Duhamel formula for <a href="#global:w-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:w-equation">[global:w-equation]</a>, with all retirement increments included, and <a href="#global:frequency-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:frequency-bound">[global:frequency-bound]</a>, <a href="#global:cold-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:cold-gain">[global:cold-gain]</a>, <a href="#global:cold-source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:cold-source">[global:cold-source]</a>, and <a href="#global:gain-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:gain-bound">[global:gain-bound]</a>, give $$\begin{aligned}
 Z_w(t)\le {}&e^{-\kappa(t-t_1)}B_0
  +\frac{C_5+C_4\theta_0}{\kappa}+J_0\\
 &+2C_3(1+\theta_0)
     \int_{t_1}^t e^{-\kappa(t-s)}e^{-\kappa(s-t_1)}Z_w(s)\,\mathrm ds.
\end{aligned}$$ Take $s_0$ small enough that $\theta_0,J_0\le1$ and discard the first exponential in the last integrand. We obtain <a id="global:scalar-bootstrap"></a>$$\label{global:scalar-bootstrap}
 Z_w(t)\le B_0+\frac{C_4+C_5}{\kappa}+1
       +4C_3\int_{t_1}^t e^{-\kappa(s-t_1)}Z_w(s)\,\mathrm ds.$$ The integral version of Gronwall’s inequality therefore gives $$\label{global:strict-improvement}
 Z_w(t)\le
 \left(B_0+\frac{C_4+C_5}{\kappa}+1\right)
       \exp\left(\frac{4C_3}{\kappa}\right)=:B_*.$$ The constants defining $B_*$ were fixed before $B$.

Here is the complete order of parameter choices. Fix the kernel and jet parameters, the narrow width factor, the Maxwellian $\mu$, and the short time $t_1$ as in the preceding sections; also fix the early growth exponent $p$ there. The bath estimate fixes $B_0$. The proof of <a href="#global:frequency" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="global:frequency">26</a> fixes $\kappa$ from $\mu$, $t_1$, and the two chosen balls, without imposing its entropy tolerance yet. The cold estimates then fix $C_3,C_4$ uniformly for sufficiently small $s_0$, while $C_5=\|P(\mu,\mu)\|_H+1$ is already fixed. Choose $$B>\max\{B_0+2,B_*+3\}.$$ For this $B$, prescribe the entropy tolerance required by <a href="#global:frequency" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="global:frequency">26</a>, and the possibly smaller entropy tolerance, hot tolerance $\zeta$, and upper bounds on $s_0$ required by <a href="#global:gain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="global:gain">27</a>. Include $\theta_0,J_0\le1$ among these requirements.

To realize the entropy tolerance, take the hole radius $\delta$ small and the bath velocity cutoff $R_\mu$ large. Their contributions to the initial relative entropy can then be made arbitrarily small. With these parameters fixed, the jet and seed contributions become arbitrarily small as $s_0\to0$, as proved in <a href="#con:entropy-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:entropy-distance">[con:entropy-distance]</a> and its derivation. To realize the prescribed $\zeta$, use <a href="#early:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="early:continuation">24</a>: choose its auxiliary time $t_b$ and horizon upper bound $T_0$ after $\delta,\zeta$, and then take $s_0$ sufficiently small for its source estimate. Decrease $s_0$ further to meet the entropy, separation, cold-gain, and retirement requirements already listed. Reducing $T_0$ when required by the early estimates preserves all later estimates. These are fixed construction parameters; none depends on $N$, on the chosen cap solution, or on its horizon $T\in(0,T_0]$.

For completeness, the strict estimate closes the bootstrap even at retirement times. At $t_1$ it starts by <a href="#global:initial-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:initial-bound">[global:initial-bound]</a>. On an interval with no retirement, the characteristic equations make the weighted norm continuous, and <a href="#global:strict-improvement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:strict-improvement">[global:strict-improvement]</a> bounds it by $B_*<B-3$ as long as it is at most $B$. Thus it cannot first reach $B$ there. At a retirement time $L_s$, apply the argument only up to the left-hand section. The cold comparison on that interval and <a href="#global:retirement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:retirement">[global:retirement]</a> give $$Z_w(L_s+)\le Z_w(L_s-)+J_s\le B_*+1<B-2.$$ This starts the bootstrap on the next interval without having assumed its conclusion across the jump. The total density and its entropy bound are unchanged by this relabeling.

Finally, at each fixed approximation only finitely many scales occur. The cold barriers bound the sum of the active colors in $H$ norm on every finite interval, with constants allowed to depend on that approximation. The uniform bound on $w$ therefore bounds the total $H$ norm on every such interval. The cutoff local existence and continuation argument from the construction excludes a finite maximal existence time. There are only finitely many retirements at that approximation, after which $F=w$. This proves global existence and <a href="#global:uniform-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="global:uniform-bound">[global:uniform-bound]</a>, with all the stated auxiliary estimates. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:compactness"></a>
### Global limits and local conservation

The uniform bounds of Proposition <a href="#prop:global-control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:global-control">28</a> allow us to remove both the collision cutoff and the truncation of the initial jets. The relevant compactness is stronger than a bound on mass and entropy: the same-position collision products are uniformly integrable, with polynomial velocity weights. This will preserve the local energy flux and rule out defects in all five collision-invariant balances. The argument extends the fixed-horizon compactness proof of (OpenAI 2026, sec. 6) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[OpenAIPeriodic](#ref-OpenAIPeriodic)] to the global approximations constructed here; we give the proof, including the averaging step.

<a id="stmt-prop-global-limits"></a>
<div id="prop:global-limits" class="proposition">

**Proposition 29**. *Fix the construction parameters supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:global-control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:global-control">28</a>, and let $b_N\to\infty$. Either the dormant family, or a family of cap solutions at one fixed horizon $T\in(0,T_0]$, has a subsequence of total densities $F_N$ converging weakly in $L^1([0,\tau]\times\Omega)$ for every $\tau<\infty$ to a nonnegative density $F$. This subsequence can be chosen so that $F_N(t)\rightharpoonup F(t)$ in $L^1(\Omega)$ at every $t\ge0$, with uniformly small polynomially weighted velocity tails on each bounded time interval.*

<a id="comp:strong-properties"></a>
*The limit has initial datum $F_0$ and is a global entropy solution obeying local conservation in the sense of Definition <a href="#def:admissible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:admissible">1</a>. More precisely, $$\label{comp:strong-properties}
 F\in C([0,\infty);L^1(\Omega)),\qquad
 \int_0^\tau\!\int_\Omega
 \langle v\rangle^k\bigl(F+Q^+(F,F)+Q^-(F,F)\bigr)
 \,\mathrm dx\,\mathrm dv\,\mathrm dt<\infty
 \quad(k\ge0,\ \tau<\infty).$$ Total mass, momentum, and energy are conserved at every time. The common early-time pointwise majorant of the dormant approximations also bounds any dormant limit.*

</div>

Throughout the proof, $T$ denotes the fixed cap horizon when the cap family is used, whereas $\tau$ denotes an arbitrary finite observation time. Constants may depend on $\tau$, but not on $N$ or $b_N$. We write $$Q_N^\pm=Q^{\pm,b_N}(F_N,F_N),\qquad
 R_{k,N}(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb R^3}\langle v\rangle^kF_N(t,x,v)\,\mathrm dv.$$ The initial convergence is supplied by Lemma <a href="#con:datum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con:datum">12</a>; the finite-approximation entropy inequalities and local conservation laws are those of Proposition <a href="#con:finite-evolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con:finite-evolution">13</a>.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Small scales and uniform integrability

Recall that a color of scale $s=s_j$ remains active until $L_s=s^{-1/2}$. Let $\mathcal A_s(t)$ be the union, in one periodic cell, of its two signs’ enlarged sets $\mathcal O_i(t)$, including all lattice copies, when $t\le L_s$; set $\mathcal A_s(t)=\varnothing$ for $t>L_s$. We use these sets for every scale, even if that scale is absent from a particular finite approximation. The active enlarged sets are disjoint. Their axial lengths on $[0,\tau]$ are bounded by $C_\tau$, so $$|\mathcal A_s(t)|\le C_\tau s^2
 \qquad(0\le t\le\tau).$$ The free-core amplitude estimate, the cold remainder bound, and the bounds on the bath and the hot or regular part imply <a id="comp:moment-envelope"></a>$$\label{comp:moment-envelope}
 R_{k,N}(t,x)\le C_{k,\tau}
 \left(1+\sum_{j\ge1}
       \frac{\mathbf 1_{\mathcal A_{s_j}(t)}(x)}{t+s_j}\right).$$ Here the bounded term includes all nonmain colors and all retired colors: after retirement they belong to $w\le BH$. For active main colors the estimate follows from Lemma <a href="#cold:core-amplitude" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cold:core-amplitude">17</a> and Proposition <a href="#cold:control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cold:control">16</a>; the nonmain contribution is controlled by Lemma <a href="#cold:nonmain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cold:nonmain">18</a>.

<a id="comp:small-annuli"></a>
<a id="comp:fixed-time-small"></a>
The square of this envelope controls the collision products. Disjointness ensures that no product of two distinct singular terms appears when it is squared, and one scale contributes at most <a id="comp:annular-square"></a>$$\label{comp:annular-square}
 \int_0^\tau\frac{C_\tau s^2}{(t+s)^2}\,\mathrm dt
 =C_\tau s^2\left(\frac1s-\frac1{\tau+s}\right)
 \le C_\tau s.$$ For $\varepsilon>0$, put $\mathcal A_{<\varepsilon}(t)=
\bigcup_{s_j<\varepsilon}\mathcal A_{s_j}(t)$. Since the scales form a geometric sequence, $$\label{comp:small-annuli}
 \sup_N\int_0^\tau\!\int_{\mathcal A_{<\varepsilon}(t)}
          R_{k,N}(t,x)^2\,\mathrm dx\,\mathrm dt
 \le C_{k,\tau}\sum_{s_j<\varepsilon}(s_j+s_j^2)
 \longrightarrow0
 \qquad(\varepsilon\downarrow0).$$ The first power of the envelope gives, uniformly at individual times, $$\label{comp:fixed-time-small}
 \sup_{N,\,0\le t\le\tau}
 \int_{\mathcal A_{<\varepsilon}(t)}R_{k,N}(t,x)\,\mathrm dx
 \le C_{k,\tau}\sum_{s_j<\varepsilon}(s_j+s_j^2)
 \longrightarrow0.$$ These estimates include retirement times, because a color may be included in its last active enlargement at that section and is covered by the bound on $w$ thereafter.

<a id="comp:fixed-majorant"></a>
Outside $\mathcal A_{<\varepsilon}(t)$, a main color can have only one of the finitely many scales $s_j\ge\varepsilon$. Their thermal widths are positive and fixed. The Gaussian velocity bounds and the summation over lattice copies therefore give $$\label{comp:fixed-majorant}
 F_N(t,x,v)\le \Psi_{\varepsilon,\tau}(v)
 \quad\text{if }x\notin\mathcal A_{<\varepsilon}(t),
 \qquad
 0\le\Psi_{\varepsilon,\tau}\le C_{\varepsilon,\tau}H.$$ The same majorant includes the bounded hot, bath, and retired parts, and the Gaussian nonmain tails. In particular it has every polynomial velocity moment; its singularity on the velocity axis is integrable.

<a id="comp:weighted-pairs"></a>
Equations <a href="#comp:small-annuli" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:small-annuli">[comp:small-annuli]</a>–<a href="#comp:fixed-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:fixed-majorant">[comp:fixed-majorant]</a> prove uniform integrability in the full spacetime and two-velocity variables of $$\label{comp:weighted-pairs}
 \langle v\rangle^k\langle v_*\rangle^k
 F_N(t,x,v)F_N(t,x,v_*),\qquad k\ge0.$$ Indeed their integrals on the removed spatial sets are bounded by <a href="#comp:small-annuli" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:small-annuli">[comp:small-annuli]</a>; on the complement they are dominated by the fixed integrable function $\langle v\rangle^k\Psi_{\varepsilon,\tau}(v)
 \langle v_*\rangle^k\Psi_{\varepsilon,\tau}(v_*)$. First make the removed contribution small, and then use absolute continuity of this fixed integral on arbitrary small measurable sets. The same decomposition gives uniformly small weighted velocity tails. Using the first power instead of the square proves the corresponding one-particle assertions, both in spacetime and, by <a href="#comp:fixed-time-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:fixed-time-small">[comp:fixed-time-small]</a>, uniformly at all individual times.

<a id="comp:collision-moments"></a>
The separate collision terms satisfy the same spacetime conclusions. Collision symmetry and conservation of the sum of squared speeds give $$\label{comp:collision-moments}
 \int_{\mathbb R^3}\langle v\rangle^kQ_N^\pm(t,x,v)\,\mathrm dv
 \le C_k R_{k+1,N}(t,x)^2.$$ For the gain, this follows by placing the velocity weight on an outgoing particle and using $\langle v'\rangle^k|v-v_*|
 \le C_k\langle v\rangle^{k+1}\langle v_*\rangle^{k+1}$; the loss is estimated directly. The cutoff factor is at most one. Thus <a href="#comp:small-annuli" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:small-annuli">[comp:small-annuli]</a> controls their weighted integrals on the removed sets. On the complement, positivity gives $$Q_N^+\le P(\Psi_{\varepsilon,\tau},\Psi_{\varepsilon,\tau}),
 \qquad
 Q_N^-\le\Psi_{\varepsilon,\tau}\nu_{\Psi_{\varepsilon,\tau}}.$$ Both majorants have every polynomial moment, either by <a href="#comp:collision-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:collision-moments">[comp:collision-moments]</a> for the fixed density $\Psi_{\varepsilon,\tau}$ or by the bounds on $H$. Consequently $\{Q_N^\pm\}$ is uniformly integrable with every fixed polynomial velocity weight. Its integrals over time intervals whose length tends to zero vanish uniformly in $N$: the small-scale contribution can first be discarded, and the remaining fixed majorants have integrals proportional to the length of the interval.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Velocity averaging with uniformly integrable sources

Weak compactness alone does not identify a product evaluated at the same spatial point. We obtain the needed compactness of velocity averages from the transport equation. The following elementary form of the averaging principle of Golse, Lions, Perthame, and Sentis (Golse et al. 1988) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[GolseLionsPerthameSentis1988](#ref-GolseLionsPerthameSentis1988)] is also proved in (OpenAI 2026, sec. 6) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[OpenAIPeriodic](#ref-OpenAIPeriodic)].

<a id="stmt-comp-averaging"></a>
<div id="comp:averaging" class="lemma">

**Lemma 30**. *Let $f_n,g_n$ be locally uniformly integrable in $L^1$ on an open region of time, space, and velocity, and suppose that $(\partial_t+v\cdot\nabla_x)f_n=g_n$ there in distributions. For every smooth compactly supported velocity test $\psi$, the averages $\int\psi(v)f_n(t,x,v)\,\mathrm dv$ are relatively compact in local $L^1_{t,x}$ wherever the hypotheses hold for all velocities in a neighborhood of $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\psi$. The spatial domain may be a flat torus or an open subset of $\mathbb R^3$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-comp-averaging"><summary>Proof of stmt-comp-averaging</summary>

*Proof.* Localize to a compact time-space set and a compact velocity neighborhood of $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\psi$, using smooth cutoffs. Denote the localized functions by $q_n$, extend them by zero, and set $r_n=(1+\partial_t+v\cdot\nabla_x)q_n$. The equation shows that $r_n$ is uniformly integrable in $L^1$ and has support in a fixed bounded set of time and velocity, and also of space in the Euclidean case. Spatial cutoff derivatives cause no difficulty because velocity has been localized. Integration from a time preceding the support gives $$q_n(t,x,v)=\int_0^\infty e^{-s}r_n(t-s,x-sv,v)\,\mathrm ds=:Rr_n(t,x,v).$$ The damped inverse $R$ is an $L^1$ contraction, since free transport preserves Lebesgue measure.

Truncate $r_n$ at height $L$, preserving its sign, to obtain $r_n^{(L)}$. Uniform integrability implies $$\sup_n\|r_n-r_n^{(L)}\|_1\longrightarrow0
 \quad(L\to\infty),\qquad
 \sup_n\|r_n^{(L)}\|_2<\infty
 \quad\text{for fixed }L.$$ The velocity averages of $R(r_n-r_n^{(L)})$ are therefore uniformly small in $L^1$. It remains to prove compactness for a bounded $L^2$ family of right-hand sides.

Take Fourier transform in $(t,x)$, with Fourier series in the torus case. For $u_n=Rr_n^{(L)}$, Cauchy–Schwarz yields $$\left|\int\psi(v)\widehat u_n(\sigma,k,v)\,\mathrm dv\right|^2
 \le I_\psi(\sigma,k)
       \int|\widehat r_n^{(L)}(\sigma,k,v)|^2\,\mathrm dv,
 \qquad
 I_\psi(\sigma,k)=\int
 \frac{|\psi(v)|^2}{1+(\sigma+k\cdot v)^2}\,\mathrm dv.$$ If $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\psi\subset\{|v|\le R_0\}$, integration in the direction of $k$ gives $I_\psi\le C_\psi/|k|$ for $k\ne0$. When $|\sigma|>2R_0|k|$, the denominator instead gives $I_\psi\le C_\psi/(1+|\sigma|^2)$. Thus $I_\psi\to0$ uniformly as $|\sigma|+|k|\to\infty$. Plancherel’s theorem shows that the averages have uniformly small high-frequency $L^2$ tails. Convolution with a fixed smooth approximate identity consequently approximates them uniformly in $L^2$. On every compact time-space set these convolutions are bounded and equicontinuous, by Cauchy–Schwarz applied to translates of the convolution kernel and its derivatives. They are relatively compact there. This proves local $L^2$, and hence local $L^1$, compactness at fixed $L$; the uniform $L^1$ error as $L\to\infty$ completes the proof. The argument applies unchanged to the dual lattice of a rotated torus. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### Identification of the collision terms

The uniform integrability and velocity tightness give a subsequence with $F_N\rightharpoonup F$ in $L^1([0,\tau]\times\Omega)$. Since $D_tF_N=Q_N^+-Q_N^-$, Lemma <a href="#comp:averaging" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="comp:averaging">30</a> applies. After extraction for a countable dense family of compact velocity tests, all smooth compact velocity averages converge strongly in local $L^1_{t,x}$ to the corresponding averages of $F$. The extension from the countable family follows from the uniform integrated mass bound.

<a id="comp:pair-limit"></a>
Set $W_N(t,x,v,v_*)=F_N(t,x,v)F_N(t,x,v_*)$. By <a href="#comp:weighted-pairs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:weighted-pairs">[comp:weighted-pairs]</a>, a further subsequence has a weak $L^1$ limit $W$. For smooth compactly supported $\psi,\chi$, $$\int\psi(v)\chi(v_*)W_N(t,x,v,v_*)\,\mathrm dv\,\mathrm dv_*
 =\left(\int\psi F_N\,\mathrm dv\right)
  \left(\int\chi F_N\,\mathrm dv_*\right).$$ The right-hand side converges in measure locally in $(t,x)$. It is uniformly integrable there: its absolute value is bounded by the integral of $|\psi(v)\chi(v_*)|W_N$, and the full pair-product bounds control this integral on small time-space sets. Vitali’s theorem therefore gives convergence in local $L^1$. It follows that $W$ and $F(t,x,v)F(t,x,v_*)$ have identical integrals against separated velocity tests, for almost every $(t,x)$. A countable determining family gives a common full-measure set; finite sums of separated tests are dense among continuous functions on compact two-velocity sets. Hence $$\label{comp:pair-limit}
 W_N\rightharpoonup F(t,x,v)F(t,x,v_*)
 \quad\text{in }L^1([0,\tau]\times\Omega\times\mathbb R^3).$$ In particular the product on the right is integrable. The uniform integrability removes time strips near $0$ and $\tau$, so interior averaging suffices. Weighted tightness extends the identification to each fixed polynomial velocity weight. One may extract simultaneously for integer moment orders; domination by a higher integer order then supplies every order $k\ge0$.

Adjoining the finite angular measure preserves weak convergence. For each $\omega$, the elastic substitution on $(v,v_*)$ is measure preserving and involutive. Consequently <a href="#comp:pair-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:pair-limit">[comp:pair-limit]</a> also gives weak convergence of the primed pair products on the collision space. On bounded input velocities the collision factor is bounded and the cutoff equals one for all large $N$. The estimates with higher velocity moments remove the complement. For the gain this uses the preserved sum of squared speeds when an output test is pulled back to the inputs. These observations identify any weak limit of the separately uniformly integrable gains and losses: <a id="comp:collision-limit"></a>$$\label{comp:collision-limit}
 Q_N^\pm\rightharpoonup Q^\pm(F,F)
 \quad\text{in }L^1([0,\tau]\times\Omega),$$ also after multiplication by any fixed polynomial velocity weight. The argument permits bounded measurable tests: after the angular integration and elastic substitution, such a test remains bounded on each truncated collision domain.

We can now pass to the unrenormalized equation, including the initial boundary term, since the initial densities converge in $L^1$. Thus $D_tF=Q(F,F)$ in distributions with datum $F_0$, and both separate collision terms have the weighted integrability in <a href="#comp:strong-properties" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:strong-properties">[comp:strong-properties]</a>. The remaining work concerns the time representative, entropy, and the local invariant balances.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### A single representative at every time

For a smooth periodic $\varphi(x,v)$ compactly supported in velocity, the cutoff path equations give $$\big|\langle F_N(t)-F_N(s),\varphi\rangle\big|
 \le\int_s^t\!\int_\Omega
 \left(F_N|v\cdot\nabla_x\varphi|
       +(Q_N^++Q_N^-)|\varphi|\right)\,\mathrm dx\,\mathrm dv\,\mathrm du.$$ The first term is bounded by $C_\varphi|t-s|$. The second has a modulus tending to zero with $|t-s|$, uniformly in $N$, by the short-time-integral conclusion above. For a countable family dense in the uniform norm on each compact velocity set, Arzelà–Ascoli therefore supplies uniform convergence of the pairings on $[0,\tau]$, after one extraction.

At any individual time, <a href="#comp:fixed-time-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:fixed-time-small">[comp:fixed-time-small]</a> and <a href="#comp:fixed-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:fixed-majorant">[comp:fixed-majorant]</a> make $\{F_N(t)\}$ relatively weakly compact in $L^1(\Omega)$. Every cluster point has the smooth pairings just obtained, and hence is the same density. Thus this one subsequence converges weakly in $L^1$ at every time, defining a representative $F(t)$ whose smooth pairings are continuous and which agrees with the spacetime limit. Weighted fixed-time tightness gives the same conclusion for moments, so the conserved cutoff mass, momentum, and energy pass at every time. In particular the initial trace of this representative is $F_0$.

Let $\Phi(A,B)=(A-B)\log(A/B)$ with its nonnegative lower-semicontinuous extension. It is jointly convex: it is the sum of the two convex perspectives $A\log(A/B)$ and $B\log(B/A)$. On any bounded collision domain, <a href="#comp:pair-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:pair-limit">[comp:pair-limit]</a> and its primed version give joint weak convergence of the two arguments of $\Phi$. Restriction to $[0,t]$ preserves this convergence for every fixed $t\in[0,\tau]$. Weak lower semicontinuity therefore applies to the integral of $|a|\Phi$; the weight can be absorbed into the arguments by $\Phi(cA,cB)=c\Phi(A,B)$ for $c\ge0$. On $|v|,|v_*|\le R$ the cutoff is absent once $b_N\ge2R$. Exhausting the velocity space yields $$\int_0^t\mathcal D(F(s))\,\mathrm ds
 \le\liminf_N\int_0^t\mathcal D^{b_N}(F_N(s))\,\mathrm ds.$$ The same convex lower-semicontinuity argument applies to $\mathcal H_M(F_N(t))$ under the weak $L^1$ convergence at that time. Combining the two nonnegative terms with the finite-approximation entropy inequality and initial entropy convergence gives <a id="comp:limit-entropy"></a>$$\label{comp:limit-entropy}
 \mathcal H_M(F(t))+\int_0^t\mathcal D(F(s))\,\mathrm ds
 \le\mathcal H_M(F_0),\qquad 0\le t\le\tau.$$ The extraction is independent of $t$; no exceptional set of time endpoints is introduced by the lower-semicontinuity argument.

For completeness, the negative part of $F\log F$ is bounded by mass, energy, and a fixed Gaussian integral. On $e^{-1-|v|^2}\le F<1$, use $-F\log F\le(1+|v|^2)F$; below that threshold, monotonicity of $-r\log r$ on $(0,e^{-1})$ gives the integrable bound $(1+|v|^2)e^{-1-|v|^2}$. The positive part is controlled by the relative entropy integrand and a multiple of $F$. Hence the absolute logarithmic integral is bounded uniformly on $[0,\tau]$.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### Strong traces, renormalization, and the invariant balances

<a id="comp:path-formula"></a>
The integrability of the unrenormalized collision operator now yields strong continuity. In free coordinates the distributional equation, including its initial trace, reads $$\partial_t\bigl[F(t,x+tv,v)\bigr]=Q(F,F)(t,x+tv,v).$$ The right-hand side belongs to spacetime $L^1$. Subtracting its Bochner integral leaves a distribution with zero time derivative and initial value $F_0$. Therefore $$\label{comp:path-formula}
 F(t,x+tv,v)=F_0(x,v)
       +\int_0^t Q(F,F)(s,x+sv,v)\,\mathrm ds$$ holds as an $L^1_{x,v}$ identity, first for almost every time. Its right-hand side is absolutely continuous in $L^1$. Free transport is a strongly continuous group on $L^1(\Omega)$, as follows by approximation by continuous functions with compact velocity support. Thus <a href="#comp:path-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:path-formula">[comp:path-formula]</a> defines a strongly continuous representative. Its smooth pairings agree almost everywhere in time with those of the representative already chosen; continuity makes them agree at every time. In particular $F\in C([0,\tau];L^1)$, $F(0)=F_0$ strongly, and pairings with every bounded measurable test are continuous. All these observations also hold with any fixed polynomial velocity weight, since both the datum and the source have that integrability.

Equation <a href="#comp:path-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:path-formula">[comp:path-formula]</a> holds on almost every individual free path. On such a path $M(v)>0$ is constant. For a renormalization $\beta$ from Definition <a href="#def:admissible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:admissible">1</a>, the ordinary absolutely continuous chain rule gives $$D_t\bigl[M\beta(F/M)\bigr]=\beta'(F/M)Q(F,F).$$ The source is integrable because $|\beta'|\le C_\beta$. Moreover, for nonnegative $f,g$, $$|M\beta(f/M)-M\beta(g/M)|\le C_\beta|f-g|,
 \qquad |M\beta(f/M)|\le C_\beta f.$$ The strong initial and terminal traces consequently pass through the renormalization. Integrating the path identity against a smooth test proves exactly <a href="#eq:renormalized" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:renormalized">[eq:renormalized]</a>, including its initial term. The separate integrability requirement follows from $0\le Q^\pm/(1+F/M)\le Q^\pm$.

Finally, let $\psi\in\{1,v_1,v_2,v_3,|v|^2/2\}$ and let $\phi\in C_c^\infty([0,\infty)\times(\mathbb R^3/\Lambda))$. At the cutoff level the local identity is $$\int_0^\infty\!\int_\Omega
 F_N\psi(v)(\partial_t\phi+v\cdot\nabla_x\phi)
 \,\mathrm dx\,\mathrm dv\,\mathrm dt
 +\int_\Omega F_N(0,x,v)\psi(v)\phi(0,x)\,\mathrm dx\,\mathrm dv=0.$$ It follows from the path equation and collision symmetry, all integrals being absolutely convergent. Weighted weak convergence of $F_N$ and weighted convergence of the initial data pass this identity to $F$. For the energy identity the largest required weight is $|v|^3$, which is covered by <a href="#comp:strong-properties" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:strong-properties">[comp:strong-properties]</a>. This proves all the exact local balances <a href="#eq:local-balances" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-balances">[eq:local-balances]</a> with their initial traces. Equivalently, their collision terms vanish in the limit by absolute weighted collision integrability; no moment defect is present.

Apply the preceding extractions successively on $[0,m]$, $m\in\mathbb N$, and take a diagonal subsequence, retaining the countable moment and velocity-test families. The representatives on overlapping intervals agree by their smooth pairings, so they define one global strongly continuous density. All integrability, entropy, and conservation statements hold on every finite interval. For a dormant sequence, sum the cold majorants over all scales and add the bath and dormant-hot majorants on their common early interval. This is a locally integrable pointwise upper bound independent of $N$. Testing against nonnegative compactly supported functions and passing to the weak limit preserves it. Its velocity integral is also integrable in space at each fixed time, by the first-power estimate used in <a href="#comp:fixed-time-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="comp:fixed-time-small">[comp:fixed-time-small]</a>. The every-time weak convergence therefore preserves the bound at each such time section as well. This completes the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:global-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:global-limits">29</a>.

<a id="sec:dilation"></a>
### The cap dilation and its linear limit

The cap selects finite solutions whose hot component has a prescribed weighted size at some time before the horizon. We now magnify that event. The bath and the quadratic hot interactions disappear under this magnification; the cold particles converge to two axial velocity atoms whose densities retain the annular geometry. This is the dilation argument of (OpenAI 2026, sec. 7) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[OpenAIPeriodic](#ref-OpenAIPeriodic)], with the additional seed factor and bath included explicitly below.

<a id="eq:rescaling"></a>
<a id="dil:cap-bound"></a>
Choose horizons $T_N\downarrow0$, terminal scales $s_N/T_N\to0$, and cutoffs $b_N\to\infty$, and take the cap solutions furnished by Proposition <a href="#early:cap-selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="early:cap-selection">22</a>. Indices may be relabeled after passage to a subsequence. Write $m_N=m_{T_N}=T_N^p$, and choose a time $t_{*,N}\in[0,T_N]$ at which the cap is attained. On the periodic lift, define $$\label{eq:rescaling}
\begin{gathered}
r_N=t_{*,N}+s_N,\qquad
h_{r_N}(t,x,v)=\frac{h_N(r_Nt,r_Nx,v)}{m_Nr_N},\qquad
C_{r_N}(t,x,v)=r_Nc_N(r_Nt,r_Nx,v),\\
l_N=\frac{s_N}{r_N}\longrightarrow l\in[0,1],\qquad
U_N=\frac{T_N}{r_N}\longrightarrow U\in[1,\infty].
\end{gathered}$$ The limits are obtained by subselection, allowing the second ratio to tend to infinity. We have $r_N\to0$, $r_N\le2T_N$ eventually, and $U_N\ge(1+s_N/T_N)^{-1}$, which proves $U\ge1$. The rescaled cap and its attained value are $$\label{dil:cap-bound}
0\le h_{r_N}(t,x,v)\le(t+l_N)H(v)\quad(0\le t\le U_N),
\qquad \|h_{r_N}(1-l_N)\|_H=1.$$ When no confusion is possible, we suppress the index on $r_N$.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### The limiting mesh and background

The dilated annular scales are $\varrho_{N,j}=s_j/r_N$, $j\leq N$. Their ratio is $e^\eta$, their smallest member is $l_N$, and their largest member tends to infinity.
